The decisions live in the pure `plan_body_substitution` because `resolve_vault_json_body` needs a live provider — three rules survived their first mutation while they lived inside the async body. **Stated limits**: substitution does not make a credential unreachable by a module that also chooses the destination (a module granted `plaid/*` and an allowed host it controls can echo it back, exactly as through a header; the bound is `allowed_hosts` ∩ `allowed_secrets`, unchanged); `resolve_vault_json_body` and the `fetch` wiring are covered by TEXTUAL pins, and two silent mutations live there — resolving the body then sending the guest's originals, and continuing past a refusal; nothing is driven against a real provider. **Consequence for `talos-plaid` (#933), recorded**: its SCOPE is now wrong rather than the crate — the two READ endpoints belong in a sandboxed, fuel-bounded module with the credential never in its address space, while `/item/public_token/exchange` must STAY controller-side because it RETURNS a long-lived credential and a module's response becomes `module_executions.output_data`.

* **EF, 2026-09-23 — three documented env vars an operator had just set reached nothing; check 97.** The controller's compose service has **no `env_file`** — its `environment:` map is an explicit `KEY: ${VAR}` list — so a variable not named there never reaches the process: the operator edits `.env`, restarts, and the feature stays off with no error, warning or log line. **The first framing was WRONG and the measurement said so**: *documented + read + untransported* is **143 of 302 (47%)**, nearly every one a TUNABLE with a working default for which no transport is CORRECT — a lint there would be 143 findings on correct code. What marks a defect is the **absence of a default** (the docs' Default cell reads `none`, so the variable is the only way to turn the feature on): **22 read by production code, 8 untransported — 5 defects and 3 with decisions already written**. Transport is **inert when unset** (`${VAR:-}` → empty → check 73), so adding one cannot change a deployment that does not set it. Plaid is controller-only (the worker is credential-free and a module reaches Plaid by EE's body substitution). Opted out with the reason each already had: the two `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_*` endpoints (check 69's `JAEGER_ENDPOINT` prohibition — they are the fallbacks `endpoint_from_env` consults, so transporting them reopens trace export through the back door) and `S3_ACCESS_KEY_ID` (the host functions answer `NotConfigured`). **Check 97**: 8 on pristine main, 0 after, 3 opt-outs. **Two findings against the check itself, both from running it**: **a COMMENT IS NOT TRANSPORT** — the NOTE saying the OTEL var is *deliberately NOT here* made it read as PRESENT, so the check shipped **green over its own documented exemption** (checks 73/87's self-report trap); comments are stripped before the transport scan and read only for the opt-out marker, and stripping them surfaced `S3_ACCESS_KEY_ID`, which the first measurement had missed. And **the any-surface rule let one deployment vouch for another** — removing `PLAID_SECRET` from `docker-compose.yml` SURVIVED because the chart still named it while the original defect was fully restored; `.env` is the COMPOSE mechanism, so the rule is now TWO legs checked separately, measured before splitting: 19 of 22 are in both families and **2 are compose+installer** (`TALOS_CONTROLLER_PUBLIC_KEY`, `TALOS_WORKER_PUBLIC_KEYS` — RFC 0010 worker trust is staged by the installer, deliberately not in the chart YAML), so the second leg accepts either. **Stated limits**: TEXTUAL, so a runtime-assembled name or a surface outside the two families is invisible; the cluster leg proves the name appears in the chart/installer family, **not** that a particular file carries it (chart parity is check 89(d)'s and render check 5's); the Default cell is prose. **One mutation is recorded INVALID rather than counted**: dropping a leg on a tree with no defect for that leg proves nothing and had to be paired with the defect.

* **EG, 2026-09-23 — linking a Plaid item, controller-side, with the credential's only destination the vault; and the KEK resolution gets ONE home.** `talos-plaid` shipped with three API methods and **zero callers** outside the boot check — the reached-is-not-reachable class. **The surface is a CONTROLLER SUBCOMMAND and that is argued**: `/item/public_token/exchange` RETURNS a long-lived credential, so it cannot be a module (a module's response becomes `module_executions.output_data`); MCP is read-only for secrets by decision (MCP-1201); a GraphQL mutation is the right home for a future Hosted Link CALLBACK but cannot run before any browser flow exists; `publish-templates` is the precedent. The decisions are PURE (`talos_plaid::link`): `check_source` REFUSES a sandbox source against a production config before any request, so production app credentials never reach an endpoint that does not exist there — the reverse is deliberately PERMITTED, since a browser token in sandbox is the one flow that proves the production shape; `access_token_path` REFUSES `/`, whitespace, non-ASCII and >128 bytes in the Plaid-chosen item id rather than sanitising, because a silently rewritten path is a credential nobody can find. `LinkReport` **has no token field**, so the renderer cannot print one by accident. **ONE home for the vault paths** under `PLAID_VAULT_PREFIX`, because the writer, the module's `vault://` references and its `plaid/*` grant must agree or the credential is written where the reader may not look — pinned by a test asserting every written path sits under the grant prefix. **All three are written**: Plaid takes `client_id` and `secret` as BODY fields on every endpoint, and EE's body substitution is the only way a guest gets a secret into one. **The KEK finding is the load-bearing part**: `SecretsManager::new` hardcodes the ENV provider and the deployment's real resolution was INLINE in `build_core_services`, so a second builder on a `KEK_PROVIDER=vault` deployment would wrap new DEKs with the wrong key while the server unwraps with the right one — which `with_kek_providers`' own doc calls silently corrupting every new DEK. **This is check 4's OWN stated reason #1 ("KEK drift") and the structural answer had never been written**: `resolve_kek_providers` is the one home, the `// prod-kek-guard` production refusal (check 45) moves with it so every caller inherits it, and the CLI calls the same function the server does. **Check 4 was comment-blind and is now comment-aware** — it fired twice on the doc comments *explaining why the shared resolution exists*, i.e. it pressured the author away from writing the sentence describing its own rule (the third instance of that trap in one day); still ZERO on pristine main, a planted real construction still caught. **PROVEN LIVE against Plaid Sandbox**: one `controller plaid-link --sandbox` linked an item with 14 accounts, wrote all three secrets at `encryption_format_version = 4` with `org_id` set, and printed **no token**. **Recorded because it would read as a bug later**: the three secrets carry `allowed_modules = {}`, which is CORRECT — `vault://` prefetch resolves as the USER, so `owner_user_id`/`org_id` govern; `allowed_modules` gates the DIFFERENT route of a module calling `secrets::get-secret` directly. **G6 SURVIVED**: the client's sandbox-only gate sat behind a method needing a network, so nothing drove it — extracted to the pure `sandbox_endpoint_permitted`. **Stated limits**: the CLI body is not driven by a test, so the exchange-before-write ordering — which exists so a failed exchange leaves the vault untouched — is argued and observed, not asserted; the production path still has no way to obtain a `public_token`.

* **EH, 2026-09-23 — the two SANDBOX surfaces handed a guest the plaintext the ENGINE never gives it; three divergences, one direction.** **The first reading was WRONG and is corrected here**: `get_rust_scaffold`'s "WASM modules MUST NOT see the plaintext secret … delivered AS-IS to your config" is TRUE in production — dispatch delivers the LITERAL and the worker resolves at the outbound call. What was false is `vault_resolver`'s own module doc, claiming "`test_module` and the engine injected plaintext … identical behavior everywhere". **The helper written to END divergence had become the divergence**, and the surface that diverged was the one putting a credential in the guest: `replace_vault_values` had exactly TWO call sites, both in `sandbox.rs`; the engine was not one of them. **Three divergences, all test-surface-only**: plaintext substituted into payload root/`config`/`input`; `retain_wire_safe_secrets` NOT applied, so a referenced OAuth **refresh token** could be fetched into a job's secret map; and the LLM-provider prefetch run **unconditionally**, so a **tier-1** actor's test job carried the very keys `build_encrypted_secrets_for` skips entirely — the defence-in-depth CLAUDE.md states as "tier-1 jobs never have an Anthropic/OpenAI/Gemini key on the wire", reachable around through the dev-test surface. MCP-692's own class: it closed the back door for the tier GATE and left it open for the key PREFETCH four lines above. **`replace_vault_values` is DELETED, not deprecated** — `check_vault_refs_resolvable` keeps the reserved-path refusal (so a refresh token is never FETCHED rather than fetched-and-dropped) and the resolvability check (a deliberate test-time EXTRA making test STRICTER than production, stated not silent) and **takes no payload parameter, so substitution is impossible by TYPE**. Both handlers apply the ENGINE's own `retain_wire_safe_secrets` (made `pub`, one home) and gate the prefetch on tier; `core_tier`'s wildcard fails CLOSED to Tier-1, because that value decides which secrets are dropped. **Blast radius measured before changing anything**: 48 nodes across 23 workflows carry a config `vault://` and **every one is an auth header**, so the pattern works precisely BECAUSE the engine delivers the literal — removing substitution breaks nothing and makes test agree with production. **Two stale docs corrected**: `test_module`'s "unless you pass the actor_id of a Tier-2 actor" has been wrong since the `egress_scope` split (**tier1 + egress_scope=public** reaches an external API while refusing every external LLM provider — proven live against `sandbox.plaid.com`), and the scaffold documented `vault://` for HEADERS only, one release behind EE. **H1 is caught by a COMPILE ERROR** because the function no longer exists — the structural answer rather than a lint. **H3 is EQUIVALENT, not a survivor**: un-gating the prefetch changes no observable behaviour because `retain_wire_safe_secrets` drops the LLM keys at tier-1 anyway, so the filter is the guarantee and the gate only saves the fetch; saying which is which matters more than claiming six of six. **Stated limits**: the handlers are pinned TEXTUALLY; and the first draft of the resolver tests passed VACUOUSLY because `extract_vault_refs` scans only the TOP LEVEL while the fixtures nested their refs under `config` — every fixture now asserts the refs were extracted first.

* **EI, 2026-09-23 — the write ceiling's HTTP and GraphQL legs INFER "mutating" from the VERB, and no operator-facing surface said so.** A Plaid module came back `http-fetch denied by policy 'write-ceiling'` and the first diagnosis — by the author of the gate — went to `allowed_hosts`; the host and methods were allowed, and what refused the call is that `/accounts/balance/get` is a **POST** while `http_method_mutates` reads "GET is the only read verb". **The control is LIVE, not latent, and the code default is the misleading half**: `write_ceiling_enforced()` is `false` when unset, but compose and `values.yaml` set it on BOTH services, so this fleet reads `enforced_by = "all"`. **Of the 16 `write_ceiling_refuses*` call sites, THREE infer and exactly ONE proves**: `http-fetch`/`http-fetch-all` classify by verb; `graphql-execute` treats every call as a mutation because the operation type is not provable from a request string; `database-query` walks the AST (#757, check 85). `webhook-send` LOOKS like an HTTP leg and is not — `record webhook-request` carries no method field, so it is always an implicit POST. **The exposure measurement corrected itself twice and the final number is the one to carry**: 114 modules, 37 declaring POST and 51 declaring nothing reads as "88 of 114 (77%) can POST", and that is WRONG because 24 are `minimal-node` and cannot make an HTTP call at all — the population is **64 of 90 HTTP-capable modules (71%)**. **The actor side is thin**: of 11 actors 6 hold `write` and 5 `readonly`, only ONE readonly actor is `active`, and its single HTTP-capable module declares `{GET}`, so it is NOT refused today; the one refusal this gate has produced on this fleet was a POST that is a READ. **Nothing about the DECISION changes** — the inference is the right fail-closed default and it over-refuses rather than under-refuses; what changes is that three surfaces now say what was INFERRED. `record_capability_denied_detailed` takes `Option<&'static str>` and APPENDS to the unchanged base line, so every existing reader still matches; `&'static str` is the point (the detail can never be caller data); **the ledger payload is deliberately UNCHANGED**, because `capability`/`policy`/`target` are the queryable fields and a free-text sentence is not one. Both constants name the WAY OUT — an operator told only "denied" widens `allowed_hosts`, which is not the control that refused. The note is appended to the two states that can REFUSE and deliberately NOT to `None`/`Unknown`; **five surfaces render it from one const** so they cannot word one fleet differently. **N8 SURVIVED and is the finding inside the finding**: rewording the fleet note's claim left everything green, because the placement test asserted `contains(the constant)` — true of ANY wording — while the tool-description test read a different literal in another crate; three surfaces agreed on a CLAIM and nothing pinned the WORDS. Closed with `WRITE_CEILING_VERB_RULE`, one home for the two CONTROLLER-side surfaces (the worker deliberately does NOT import it — a worker→repository-crate edge is check 51's direction — so its detail keeps a second literal with its own assertion and a comment saying why). **One mutation is recorded INVALID**: its first needle matched TWICE, including the control assertion that must NOT contain it. **Stated limits**: the three gates are NOT driven end to end, because `write_ceiling_enforced()` is a process-global `OnceLock` a sibling test in the same binary decides (check 82's own objection); the pin's needles are ASSEMBLED, because the first draft matched its own line; and it scans the WHOLE file rather than a production region, because `graphql.rs` carries a `#[cfg(test)]` module 400 lines above its gate. **No lint check was added, `--count` stays 97**: *"an inferring gate must pass a detail"* is a population of THREE already covered by the pin; *"every call site must declare inferred-vs-proven"* ships at **13 markers on correct code** over 16 sites and is 0-for-0 as a bug detector. **Deliberately NOT done**: no SOC 2 control row for the write ceiling (asserting a new control is a compliance claim needing its own decision), and the dated 2026-07-20 injection audit is left as written.

* **EJ, 2026-09-24 — `allowed_methods` was the one module grant where declaring nothing granted everything; ONE home now, and a fifth gate that had none.** A module carries THREE grants and two denied on empty (`allowed_hosts` outright; `allowed_secrets` via `vault_path_permitted`) while `allowed_methods` **admitted every verb**, in FOUR hand-rolled copies — and the correct shape sat in the SAME FILE, `webhook.rs` denying an empty HOST list twelve lines above where it admitted an empty METHOD list. ONE home `talos_workflow_job_protocol::method_permitted`, beside `vault_path_permitted`, because both binaries hold module grants and must agree what one means. **And a FIFTH surface had no gate at all**: `http_stream::connect` never consulted `allowed_methods`, which mattered the instant the other four denied — "an undeclared module cannot make HTTP calls" would have been FALSE. An SSE connect is a GET (the WIT `connect(url, headers)` carries no method). **NO WILDCARD, and the reason is measured**: `allowed_secrets` needs `["*"]` because the vault key space is unbounded; the method space is **CLOSED at five** (`wit/talos.wit`'s `enum method`), so "every verb" is five strings — which the one module on this fleet using every verb already writes. A wildcard would add a second spelling to a bounded set and quietly re-create allow-by-accident; `a_star_is_not_a_wildcard_here_unlike_allowed_secrets` pins BOTH halves including the CONTRAST that `*` *is* documented for secrets. **Blast radius measured ZERO in three independent populations**: of **27** installed modules above `Minimal` with an empty list, **0** call any HTTP surface; of **29** `TalosContext::new` sites, 24 pass an empty list and exactly ONE is production, whose `allowed_hosts` is `vec![]` too and whose host gates run BEFORE the method gate — left empty DELIBERATELY, since deny-all on both axes is what that path means; of **75** shipped catalog templates (the population that speaks for OTHER deployments) 44 call HTTP and all 44 declare methods, **0 break**. **Not zero in general, and the refusal says so**, naming `update_module_methods` (`METHOD_ALLOWLIST_REMEDY`, one home, pinned). **My own instrument was wrong once and the contradiction is the lesson**: the first catalog scan reported **10 templates would break** against the DB's zero, because it concatenated the GENERATED `src/bindings.rs`, which declares every host interface of the world whether the module calls it or not. Two contradicting measurements mean re-read the instrument; the loud direction is not automatically the true one. **Seven prose sites swept**, including the OPERATOR-FACING `update_module_methods` description, which would have told an operator the opposite of what the code does — and `methods_are_read_only`'s doc comment, whose VERDICT is unchanged and still right for the OPPOSITE reason (an undeclared module makes no HTTP call, so there is nothing for a transient retry to re-send). **The behaviour-change proof is a test that already existed**: `post_not_in_allowed_methods_is_refused` carried, as a CONTROL, the assertion that an empty list PASSES — the old rule written down. **The first 23 fixtures were the whole population my enumerator could see, and that is the miss worth recording**: test-vs-production was classified with `strip_test_modules`, which finds a `#[cfg(test)]` region inside a `src/` file and is structurally blind to a `tests/`-dir binary in a DEPENDENT crate — so 29 is one crate's count and the workspace holds **34**. CI found two, the more informative being a POSITIVE CONTROL whose own panic message blames the tier gate, so its failure would have sent a reader to the wrong control. Proof is pre/post against the real tree, with per-binary pass counts (36→37, 7→8) showing no test flipped the other way. **The gate-sequence lesson is the general one**: `cargo test -p <touched crates>` plus `cargo check --workspace --all-targets` COMPILES a dependent crate's test binaries and never RUNS them. **No lint check was added and `--count` stays 97**: *"a file with an egress gate must name the shared predicate"* was BUILT and measured at **4 on pristine main, 0 on the fixed tree, 100% precision** — rejected anyway because the population is FOUR files and it is FILE-scoped, so a comment naming the predicate satisfies it while a gate hand-rolls the test. **Plus the one-line EI correction**: `WRITE_CEILING_VERB_DETAIL` ended at "raise the actor's write ceiling deliberately" — the misleading-report class inside the fix for it, since `max_write_ceiling` is ONE scalar over fifteen ops and raising it to clear one INFERRED HTTP refusal also clears every CATEGORICAL op the world grants (measured for `http-node`: `webhook-send`, `email-send`, `messaging-publish`/`-request`). The sentence does NOT imply those are equally exposed, because measuring said otherwise — `webhook-send` stays bounded by `allowed_hosts` and `email-send` is refused above that gate for a tier-1 actor, leaving the NATS publish as the one with no other control.

* **EK, 2026-09-24 — EJ made an empty `allowed_methods` deny, and five carriers were still writing one; `run_sandbox` could not issue a single HTTP request for nine hours.** **Two of EJ's own claims are corrected here** (its bullet stays byte-identical; check 88's precedent): "exactly ONE production `TalosContext::new` site, unreachable past its own deny-all" read the hardcoded pair in `execute_module_string_with_context_and_timeout` — true there, and that is `test_module`'s path — and missed that `run_sandbox` reaches `execute_job_with_full_features`, where **hosts are a caller parameter** while methods were hardcoded `vec![]`; and "swept the OPERATOR-FACING `update_module_methods` description" swept a schema property in `modules.rs`, while the TOOL is declared once, in `sandbox.rs`, and still read "Empty list = allow all methods" — so an operator arriving from `METHOD_ALLOWLIST_REMEDY` was told the opposite of the rule that refused them. **The carriers**: five writers build a module row from a template and FOUR dropped `allowed_methods` while carrying `allowed_hosts` — `compile_template`, `createModuleFromTemplate` (the twin check 68 was written about, forgotten again), `restore_pinned_modules`, the replay service's reconstruction. ONE home `talos_registry::InheritedGrants` + `NodeTemplate::inherited_grants()`, so the four grants travel as one value. **`run_sandbox` gains `allowed_methods` defaulting to ALL FIVE** (`ALL_HTTP_METHODS`, `sandbox_default_methods`), operator decision 2026-09-24, and it is the one grant in this family that defaults open: the caller supplies the code AND the host list in the same call, so the method axis bounds nothing the host axis does not, `allowed_hosts` still defaults deny-all, and defaulting closed left the tool unable to issue any request with no parameter to open it. An EXPLICIT `[]` is honoured verbatim (how a caller rehearses the installed posture) and the schema states the asymmetry. **Live fleet NOT broken, measured**: 4 of 113 modules carry hosts-with-no-verbs and all four are non-egress trigger/parsers whose `fetch_history` is a stub (`network-scanner` uses raw `wasi:sockets`, which this gate does not cover); they trace to 4 of 75 catalog manifests, copied faithfully. **Deliberately NOT changed**: tightening those four manifests is a least-privilege review of the catalog, not a carrier fix — and **deliberately NO seed-time WARN**, which would fire 4× at every boot on a fleet where the state is benign (check 69's trap). **Recorded, latent**: `parse_manifest_allowed_methods` DROPS unrecognised verbs rather than failing, so a manifest declaring only `HEAD` would seed a silently deny-all row — 0 of 75 do today. **17 stale prose sites, not the 7 EJ claimed**, the population growing TWICE during the sweep — including `talos-failure-analysis-service`'s remediation advice for `method_not_allowed`, i.e. the platform's own recovery instructions told the operator that clearing the list grants everything. EJ's sweep followed the CODE change and the prose lives in crates the code never touched. **The pin is measured in both directions and its first draft was rejected on measurement**: a ±3-line window reported **3 of 4** on pristine main — it missed the GraphQL twin, whose two fields sit NINE lines apart, i.e. exactly the site most likely to be forgotten; scanning the whole struct literal by brace balance gives **4 of 4 on `fef2c163`, 0 after**. The pin lives in **its own file** deliberately: its first draft sat inside one of the three files it `include_str!`s, so reverting that file to reproduce the defect deleted the test with it. A second pin covers the `run_sandbox` CALL SITE, which no resolver test can see (checks 74b/79b). **No lint check was added, `--count` stays 97**: five writers in three files, below #765's bar.

* **EL, 2026-09-24 — `max_write_ceiling` splits into two axes, and the partition is PROVABILITY rather than destination.** Of the FIFTEEN gate sites, **THREE** infer "is this a mutation?" from the HTTP verb and **TWELVE** do not have to; the inferring three over-refuse BY DESIGN (EI), and that over-refusal is the only part of the ceiling an operator has a principled reason to override separately. **A DESTINATION split was measured and REJECTED** (operator decision 2026-09-24): it puts `email-send`, `webhook-send` and `messaging-publish` on the same axis as `http-fetch`, so an actor granted the override to make POST-shaped READS would also gain mail and NATS — three categorical side effects nothing was over-refusing. **Live evidence**: `plaid-read` is `http-node`, `allowed_methods={POST}`, one host, calls `http::fetch` and nothing else; its actor holds `write` solely because Plaid's reads are POSTs. **And the split's live reach is ONE of five write actors, stated plainly** — the other four all need `data=write`, because every data mutation on this fleet goes through the `__memory_write__` ENVELOPE (measured: 8 modules use it, **zero** call `agent_memory::set`/`integration_state`/`database`/`object_storage`/`messaging`/`email`/`webhook` directly). The value is forward-looking: every future API-reader actor. **`actors.http_verb_ceiling`** (migration `20260924120000`) is NULLABLE with NO DEFAULT deliberately — a default would make "inherit" indistinguishable from "explicitly set to the inherited value", and the three-valued read is what lets the signed field be a CONDITIONAL APPEND (`:hvc=`), so a default-shaped job is byte-identical and **no coordinated controller+worker restart is needed** (unlike `max_write_ceiling` itself, bound unconditionally). The column carries **its own escalation trigger**, copied from `20260709180000`: a guard covering one of two escalation paths is not a guard. **ONE home**: `talos_workflow_engine_core::{CeilingAxis, effective_write_ceiling, write_ceiling_denies_axis, narrow_verb_inference_override, http_verb_ceiling_from_db}`, re-exported through `talos-workflow-job-protocol` because `talos-worker-runtime` reaches core only through it. **The axis is a REQUIRED parameter of `write_ceiling_refuses`**, so a new gate site cannot inherit the wrong half by saying nothing. **Narrowing has its own rule**: `None` means INHERIT, so `Option::or` would let a parent's `Some(Write)` win over a child's `Some(ReadOnly)`; `narrow_verb_inference_override` takes the more restrictive of two explicit values and never lets an absent side widen an explicit one. **The fail-closed stamp writes an EXPLICIT `ReadOnly` on the new axis, never `None`** — `None` would be restrictive only by coincidence of the ceiling beside it. **FOUR mutations SURVIVED the first run**, all the call-site/plumbing class: the fail-closed stamp left inheriting, the repository collapsing SQL NULL into `Some(Write)` (which would have granted POST to EVERY actor), the graphql gate reverting to the categorical axis, and — the worst — a CATEGORICAL gate (`email-send`) moved onto the verb axis, i.e. the override granting mail, the exact thing the chosen partition exists to prevent. Closed by `ceiling_axis_pins`, which DERIVES the `(op, axis)` pairs from the nine host files and asserts the verb axis covers EXACTLY the three, with a floor so a scan that stops matching fails. **A pre-existing gap was found by the new wire snapshot**: `sign_request_with_fixed_nonce` had never mirrored `:egress=` or `:idem=`, invisible because every snapshot left those at their defaults. **`--count` stays 97.**

* **EM, 2026-09-24 — EL's axis was a NO-OP: the worker dropped the override on arrival, and four operator surfaces still advised the broad grant.** EL added the column, the recorded setter, the signed wire field, the decision, the narrowing, the axis assignment at all fifteen gate sites, four pins and nine mutations — and `talos-worker-runtime` never copied the value from the job onto the context. `TalosContext::http_verb_ceiling` was initialised `None` and READ by the gate, so the override was signed, travelled, arrived and was discarded; every actor's verb gate still read `max_write_ceiling`. Measured: `context.max_write_ceiling` is assigned at 2 runtime sites and threaded through 4 signatures; `context.http_verb_ceiling` was assigned at **0**. **Why nine mutations missed it**: a mutation can only change code that EXISTS, and the defect was an ABSENT assignment; `ceiling_axis_pins`' siblings check which axis each gate NAMES, not whether the value arrives. The CARRIER class for the third time in one session — EJ flipped a grant, EK found five carriers dropping it, EL's own plumbing dropped the next one. The fix threads it at 4 signatures / 2 assignments / 2 worker-binary dispatch paths, and **`test_module` now reads the actor's override too, because it is a REHEARSAL**: reading only `max_write_ceiling` there refuses a POST the same actor's real dispatch permits. `run_sandbox` keeps `None` deliberately (no real actor binding). **Four operator surfaces carried advice EL made wrong**, the sharpest line appearing twice verbatim: *"The ceiling CANNOT express 'may POST, but only reads'"* — true when EI wrote it, false since EL. Rewritten at `WRITE_CEILING_VERB_DETAIL`, `WRITE_CEILING_VERB_NOTE` (five fleet surfaces), `set_actor_write_ceiling`'s description, and `analyze_execution_failure`'s remediation step, which now points at `set_actor_http_verb_ceiling` first; each names the narrow route AND keeps the broad one priced (`all fifteen`). **THREE mutations survived first**: `context.http_verb_ceiling = None` satisfied a pin that counted the assignment alone — the line present, carrying nothing, the exact defect restored — so the needle now names the parameter on the right-hand side; and the fleet note had no pin at all. **Measured and NOT changed**: `execution_events.error_class` is NULL on all **133 470** rows and has THREE live producers — the dispatcher's `retry_skipped` (which has NEVER fired despite 32 retry-enabled nodes, so every classified error on this fleet is transient), `extract_non_transient_class` on `node_failed`, and DX's liveness path (no liveness event since it deployed). LATENT, not dead; three of my own readings of it were wrong before the fourth was right, twice from truncating my own evidence with `head`. **`--count` stays 97.**
* **EN, 2026-09-24 — `allowed_secrets` has TWO vocabularies and four operator surfaces taught the one that delivers nothing.** The engine passes the grant list VERBATIM as `extra_paths` to `get_secrets_by_paths`, whose non-wildcard query is `WHERE key_path = ANY($1)` — **exact equality**, with only the literal `"*"` special-cased. So PERMISSION (`vault_path_permitted`: exact · bare prefix · `/*` glob · `*`) and DELIVERY (exact · `*`) are different sets: a prefix or glob entry permits a path and prefetches nothing, an empty result is `Ok`, and nothing logs or fails until the module asks. **My own first diagnosis was WRONG** (I blamed empty `allowed_modules`). **Measured fleet-wide: 63 grant entries — 8 exact, 30 naming a path this deployment has not stored, 25 that permit correctly and deliver nothing (19 glob + 6 bare prefix). The NODE-level measurement corrected itself and the correction is the instructive half**: a first pass joined on `n->'data'->>'module_id'` with the config at `n->'data'->'config'` and returned ZERO affected nodes, which I nearly recorded as *latent* — but a node's config IS `data` and its module id is `type`, so the join matched nothing and the zero was for the wrong reason. Re-measured: **31 live nodes ARE bound to a permit-only-granted module and every one carries a `vault://` reference in its own config**, so all 31 are delivered by the other route and work; the dangerous quadrant — permit-only grant AND no config reference — held exactly TWO nodes, both `plaid-read`, both failing, and now holds ZERO (fixed by naming its three exact paths, also a TIGHTER allowlist than the glob it replaced). Those 31 correct uses are the empirical case for disclosure over refusal. ONE home `talos_workflow_job_protocol::vault_path_prefetched`, the delivery twin beside `vault_path_permitted`, pinned by `prefetched_is_strictly_narrower_than_permitted` — the invariant that keeps the asymmetry SAFE — with a vacuity guard. `test_secret_access`, whose stated purpose is identifying which gate is responsible, gains a fifth gate `dispatch_prefetch`; its four existing gates ALL PASSED over a path no dispatch would deliver. **DECIDED: gate 5 is deliberately NOT folded into `would_succeed`** — a path the grant does not prefetch can still arrive by a `vault://` reference in the node's own config, the route 31 live nodes use and one this tool cannot see (it takes a module and a path and no node), so folding it would flip `would_succeed` false for modules that demonstrably work. **Deliberately NOT refusing or rewriting a glob at the write** — a glob is a legitimate permission boundary for a config-ref module (every OAuth integration is one), so the fix is DISCLOSURE. The sentence has ONE home, `SECRET_GRANT_DELIVERY_NOTE` (EI's N8 lesson), carried by all four surfaces, with gate 3's per-path remedy pinned NEGATIVELY because a count pin over static descriptions cannot see a runtime sentence. **A MORE SEVERE second finding in the same tool, DECIDED to ship in this package rather than first as its own PR.** Gate 1 tested one of the TWO routes a secret takes: the GUEST route (`secrets::get_secret()`) and not the HOST route (a `vault://` marker resolved at the socket — the safer route, and the one every OAuth integration uses). **Measured: ALL 38 live nodes carrying a `vault://` config reference are `http-node`, across 14 modules — so gate 1 was 0-for-38 on this fleet**, returning `would_succeed: false` with the remedy "Recompile with capability_world: secrets-node", which would have WIDENED 14 modules' capability world and fixed nothing; `plaid-read` was fetching 14 accounts and 49 transactions at the moment the tool called it incapable. ONE home `talos_capability_world::world_allows_vault_substitution`, whose correctness is **DERIVED from `wit/talos.wit` rather than transcribed** — `the_substitution_predicate_matches_the_wit` reads each world's own body and asserts the predicate equals "imports http/webhook/graphql", with a vacuity floor, so a world added to the lattice cannot drift it. An unknown world is claimed to have NEITHER route. Gate 1 reports per-route answers in `gates[0].routes` and keeps an actionable recompile remedy for the genuinely-unreachable `(false, false)` case, pinned as a CONTROL. **The single-package decision is stated rather than silently taken**: the standing rule is that a more severe finding ships first as its own PR, and these two are the same defect — *this tool's verdict does not describe how secrets actually reach a module* — in the same function, response contract and pin file. **No lint check was added and `--count` stays 97**: one predicate and four description sites, below #765's bar. **Stated limits**: the pins are TEXTUAL and `handle_test_secret_access` is not driven end to end, so a gate that decides correctly and renders wrongly is caught by the pin alone; the 30 entries naming an unstored path are deliberately left alone — a module declaring a path this deployment has not provisioned is a steady state, so warning at dispatch would fire on a healthy fleet (check 69's trap). **Unrelated pre-existing flake, called out not fixed**: the protocol crate's `the_retention_window_is_never_narrower_than_the_widest_verifier` failed once in six full-suite runs on this branch and zero times in three on pristine main — two tests depend on the process-global `JOB_NONCE_CACHE`'s SIZE while 59 `.verify()` call sites mutate that global without taking `NONCE_CACHE_TEST_SERIAL`; test-only, pre-existing, and the honest fix restructures a security test's relationship to a global. **Also here, the live proof owed since #941**: the EL/EM verb axis demonstrated end to end — same actor, same module, same workflow, three runs — `write`/inherit → completed; `readonly`/inherit → REFUSED `reason_class=write-ceiling`; `readonly`/**`write`** → completed (14 accounts, 49 transactions). The only change between runs 2 and 3 is the axis EL added, and the resting posture it makes expressible — `readonly` categorical + `write` verb + tier1 + egress public — is the strongest this platform can state for a POST-based financial reader.

* **EO, 2026-09-24 — a LaunchAgent PATH derived from the installing shell, and the off-host drill leg that could never have run.** `--source artifact` fails at escrow because the installed `op` is 2.16.1 and service accounts arrived in 2.18 (operator-owned: upgrade, or switch to `TALOS_OFFHOST_AGE_PASSPHRASE_FILE`); `--source b2` had never run and structurally could not, because **the scheduler propagated neither the B2 credentials nor the age passphrase into the plist**, so the leg that proves an off-host restore was unreachable while `make drill-schedule-status` said `✓ scheduled`. ONE home `scripts/lib/offhost-env.sh` (`OFFHOST_PLIST_ENV_VARS` + `render_offhost_plist_env`), read by both schedulers, so the drill and the backup agree on what an off-host run needs. `SCHEDULED_TOOLS` gains `aws` only when `DRILL_SOURCE == b2`, after validation, so an artifact-source install is not refused for a tool it never calls. `assert_pair_exclusive` now applies to BOTH passphrase pairs inside `resolve_scheduled_path` (it had covered one). **Decisions**: propagate the COMMAND or the PATH, never the key — `_CMD`/`_FILE` travel, the passphrase itself never enters a plist; and **`AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` IS propagated** while the secret is not, matching `aws.rs`'s own precedent ("the key id may be logged; the secret may not") — I had excluded it "for symmetry", which was wrong against the twin. **My grep missed all three `TALOS_OFFHOST_B2_*` vars because `[A-Z_]` excludes the digit in `B2`** — the README caught it, and without that the fix would have moved the failure from "no passphrase" to "no bucket". **Third recorded character-class miss.** **Guards**: `scripts/tests/launchd-path-test.sh` gains a b2 block with a parity loop over `OFFHOST_PLIST_ENV_VARS`, so a variable added to the list without a renderer fails; it SKIPS LOUDLY on Linux for the `plutil` half. **The harness tripwire caught MY test**: the parity probe set both halves of the passphrase pair, tripping the guard under test. **Stated limits**: the plist proves a tool RESOLVES and a variable is PRESENT, not that the credential is valid; nothing re-derives after install; the escrow leg is still operator-owned. **Retention consequence recorded**: `module_executions` went 57 755 rows / 193 MB → 69 331 / 234 MB in 11 days (~1 050 rows, 3.7 MB/day) with `MODULE_EXECUTION_RETENTION_ENABLED` off by decision (AT), the database at 888 MB.

* **EP, 2026-09-24 — the post-DN package bullets drifted 3.6× past the ceiling DN left, and the discipline that let them is now written down.** Measured before designing: CLAUDE.md was 631,346 bytes (~157k tokens at every session start and every agent brief). DN compressed 101 bullets (C..DM) to a median of **1,166 B, max 2,155**; the **26** written since (DO..EO) had a median of **4,243 B and a max of 9,373** — 26 recent bullets weighing as much as the 101 DN compressed, and **the six largest are all mine from one session**. DN set the discipline by DOING it and nothing held the line, which is check 64's "a sweep is a snapshot, not a gate" applied to this file. **25 of the 26 exceed DN's own max** (DQ alone was already inside it) and are rewritten to decisions only — every DECIDED / deliberately NOT / REJECTED / measured population / latent claim / stated limit / one-home identifier / migration id / check number kept, the discovery and mutation narrative dropped — with the originals moved VERBATIM and contiguous to the review archive under their own dated heading. **Yield stated plainly rather than rounded up: the bullet block went 115,027 → 70,508 B, a 39% cut — and the FILE went 631,346 → 593,565 B, a NET 37.8 KB / 6%, because this entry itself costs ~3.6 KB. Reporting the 39% or the gross 44.5 KB as the file's saving would be this file's own misleading-report class. ~9k tokens off every session start, and NOT the 72% DN achieved.** I could not reach DN's median by rewriting in the same register without dropping decisions, and a second tightening pass moved only 900 B; saying so matters more than claiming the precedent's number. **`BASES` gains `6b7cd9f3f554209dc43be21c79d80a4e02d0a146`** (full 40 chars — GitHub serves fetch-by-SHA only for the full id), so check 96 verifies this split's three legs against it forever; every leg runs against every base, so the two earlier splits' proofs are untouched. **DELIBERATELY NO byte-cap lint check, and `--count` stays 97**: a hard cap is the exact pressure that made DN's own chunk 3 drop decisions to fit, so the HARD rule is losslessness (check 96, already enforced) and the byte budget is a SOFT target — a bullet that needs 3 KB to keep its decisions gets 3 KB. The rule for the next session: **a package bullet is a digest at ~1,200–2,000 B; if yours is longer, the surplus is discovery or mutation narrative and belongs in the archive file.** **Deliberately NOT touched, and DN's reason is inherited rather than re-litigated**: the 97 lint checks (186 KB, 29% of the file) — but the claim that "the inline documentation IS each check's specification" is now MEASURED as only partly true and recorded for the next package: the top 12 checks are 51% of that block (74 = 14.5 KB, 88 = 10.7, 83 = 10.5, 65 = 9.8) while a check's actual specification — rule, scope, opt-out marker, measured precision, stated limits — is 600–900 B; the surplus is the story of the regression it came from, which is what this file's own split rule sends to the archive. That is a separate measured change with its own blast radius, not a side effect of this one.
* **EQ, 2026-09-24 — header substitution reached every egress surface; BODY substitution reached one of four.** Measured: `resolve_vault_header` is called from eight files, `resolve_vault_json_body` from ONE — so on `http::fetch_all` (the batch sibling in the **SAME FILE**), `webhook::send` and `graphql::execute` a marker in a body was **neither substituted nor refused: it was transmitted verbatim**, and EE made an unaddressable pointer a REFUSAL precisely because the vault PATH names the provider and, for `oauth/gmail/<uid>/<email>/…`, the account address. **Our own docs invited it** — the scaffold claims body resolution unqualified and EN's gate-1 reason repeats it for any world importing http/**webhook**/**graphql**, i.e. **90 of 115 modules**. **LATENT**: 0 of 115 modules call webhook/graphql and the one `fetch_all` caller carries no marker, so the documentation is what made this worth fixing rather than recording. **DECIDED: substitute, not refuse** — both close the leak, the docs already promise it, and it adds no blast radius (the bound stays `allowed_hosts` ∩ `allowed_secrets`). ONE resolver, four call sites; new ledger surfaces `webhook-json-body` / `graphql-json-body`, `fetch_all` reusing `HttpJsonBody` as its headers reuse `HttpHeader`. `webhook` resolves ONCE **before** the retry loop (a per-attempt resolve re-reads the vault; a rotation mid-retry splits one delivery across two credentials) and **after** the dedup fingerprint, which stays on the PLACEHOLDER deliberately — resolved bytes would put plaintext in the idempotency record and break on rotation — and REFUSES a marker in a body it does not declare JSON, since `send` sets no content type of its own. GraphQL serializes its envelope ONCE in a **scoped block**, so `.json(&body)` is a COMPILE error; `fetch`'s resolved bytes now SHADOW the original, closing the first of EE's two recorded silent mutations. Dry-run resolves on `fetch_all` and not `fetch`/`webhook` — correct either way (no egress) and pre-existing in the header path. **Deliberately EXCLUDED, and that is the decision**: host-composed bodies (`email::send` MIME, `object_storage` signed S3, `llm::*` envelopes) neither substitute nor refuse — a marker there is prose the guest chose to send, and substituting would put a secret in an email or an LLM prompt; one home, `VAULT_BODY_SUBSTITUTION_SURFACES_NOTE`. **Proof is pre/post against the real pre-fix tree, not mutation**: 4 of 11 tests fail with the four files reverted, while `fetch`'s own refusal test PASSES — the control that the suite is not green because everything refuses. The refusal is observable as the latched `reason_class` with no network, so this is a behavioural WIRING guard where EE had only a pin, and a live-provider test closes EE's "cannot be driven from a unit test". EE's pin is SUPERSEDED and moved OUT of `http.rs` (a pin must not live in a file it `include_str!`s — EK); its first replacement hit EI's `split_once("#[cfg(test)]")` truncation and then the self-report trap, for the fourth time (73, 87, 97, EG's check 4). **Mutations 10 applied, 10 caught after TWO survived**, both guard gaps rather than fix gaps: a test that refused for the wrong reason, and resolve-then-send-the-pre-substitution-bytes. **Stated limits**: `fetch_all` iterates `&reqs`, so the original stays reachable and a reverted push is caught by the pin alone; no test drives a real socket, so what reqwest was HANDED is pinned, not observed. **No lint check was added and `--count` stays 97**: *a `host/` file that sends a body must name the resolver* measures **8 on main, 2 real (25%), 6 on the fixed tree all legitimate**, and being FILE-scoped it is green over `fetch_all` — one of the three defects it was written for.

* **ER, 2026-09-25 — four lint-check entries compressed to specification only; the block is now CLOSED and EP's estimate is REFUTED.** Checks **74**, **88**, **83** and **65** kept their rule, scope, opt-out marker, every measured precision number, their recorded decisions and their stated limits; the regression STORY moved VERBATIM to [`2026-09-25-lint-check-narratives.md`](docs/engineering-log/2026-09-25-lint-check-narratives.md). Measured **45,560 → 20,993 B, a 54% cut**, and the FILE went 597,543 → ≈577,100 B (**≈20.4 KB / 3.4% net** — the digest subsection and this bullet are the difference between the 24.6 KB the entries lost and what the file actually saved). **DECIDED: no further entry is compressed.** EP implied ~85 KB recoverable from a "600–900 B" specification floor; that floor came from the MEDIAN entry (659 B) and does not survive the large ones, where the scope is a twenty-term glob, the precision is four separately-measured populations and the limits are five enumerated evasions. Per entry, worst-first: 74 **56%**, 83 **62%**, 88 **51%**, 65 **45%** — then **79 at 27%** and **85 at 12%**, both two legs plus a sub-leg with their own numbers, i.e. almost pure specification, so **79 and 85 are deliberately NOT compressed** and the remaining 91 entries sit at the floor already. DN's original reason for leaving this block alone holds better than EP's re-measurement suggested; what EP got right is that the four largest carried movable story. **`BASES` gains `7457bdbd7520cf5c560052ec04b7ec2163f5aaf5`**, so check 96 verifies this split's three legs forever (leg 1 10 removed lines / 0 missing, leg 3 1 run / 0 broken, leg 2 3 markers / 0 unrepresented). **Stated limit**: what stayed is the compressing author's judgement of specification-versus-story, so the archive keeping every original byte-for-byte is what makes a clause cut in error recoverable rather than lost. The full decision record is the `###` subsection above. **`--count` stays 97** — no check changed behaviour.

* **ES, 2026-09-25 — an unreadable watch lookup ACKED the push, discarding a delivery the transport would have redelivered; the reference integration had it right and both copies inverted it.** A push handler's watch lookup is three-valued and the two non-`Found` answers need OPPOSITE status codes: `Ok(None)` is determinate, so ACK (200) — deferring would redeliver for the life of the subscription; `Err` means existence is UNKNOWN, so DEFER (503) — acking tells the transport it was handled and **nothing else retries it**. Measured across the three transports: **Calendar** (the first integration, the one `docs/integration-pattern.md` is distilled from) answered 200 / **500** — correct; **Gmail** and **GCP**, written second and third by copying the pattern, answered **200 for both**. **Check 79 is structurally green over this by its own stated limit** — *"it proves the arms are SPLIT, never that the `Err` arm is right"* — so this is the live instance of a recorded limit, and 79's entry had also predicted the rule would fail to replicate to the next integration. **LATENT**: the arm has never fired in retained logs — but the volume behind it is the platform's busiest inbound path (`pa-ask-email` was **5 805 of 11 275 executions over the 30 days to 2026-09-25, 51%**), so each drop is an inbound question nobody sees. **ONE home** `talos_integration_helpers::push_ack::{PushWatchLookup, defer_unreadable_watch}`; 503 chosen to match the execution-pause gate **in the same handlers**, and the ordering is itself a finding — this lookup sits ABOVE that gate, so on a database outage it is reached FIRST and the pause gate's own deferred-not-dropped reasoning is unreachable in exactly the scenario it was written for. **Calendar is deliberately NOT rewritten** (its 500 already defers; churning a correct handler is an unrelated behaviour change) and is pinned in its own vocabulary instead. `talos_google_push_deferred_total{integration,reason}` is deliberately SEPARATE from the refusal counter — every `PushRefusalReason` means *we rejected this sender*, a deferral means *we could not answer one* — closed `PushDeferReason`, both pairs pre-seeded (the arm has never fired, so an unseeded series would read absent, not zero); **no alert, no baseline**. `PushIntegration` is re-exported from `push_ack` so neither handler needs a `talos-metrics` edge of its own (`talos-gmail` has none — DX's refusal of that edge stands). **Mutations 7 applied, 7 caught**, including M4 (deferring an ABSENT watch, i.e. redelivering forever), so the fix is bounded in both directions. **Two pre-existing GCP handler tests were ASSERTING THE DEFECT**, one of them justifying its 200 as "no 404 oracle" — which conflates *do not leak whether a token is valid* (correct; a 503 leaks nothing either) with *ack the delivery* (the defect). Their scaffolding (forged-valid JWT + dead pool) is what makes a REAL guard reachable, so GCP's deferral is now driven end to end through `pubsub_push_handler` with the counter asserted. **Stated limit, and it is ASYMMETRIC**: `talos-gmail` has no handler test module at all, so its call site is covered by the TEXTUAL pin alone; and neither transport covers the ABSENT (`Ok(None)`) arm, which needs a migrated database with no matching row — the corrected test comment says so, because a reader would otherwise take the dead-pool test for coverage of both. `docs/integration-pattern.md` §2 gains the rule as a table, beside the §3 `check_module_binding` row that already said `Unreadable` → 503. **`--count` stays 97.**

**Deliberately NOT done / recorded.** (A post-auth DLQ enqueue site was listed here and is CLOSED — #796's `capture_post_auth_drop` stamps every below-the-gate dispatch failure `authenticated: true`; the pre-auth breaker/rate-limit drops stay un-replayable by design.) (TOTP/OTLP AAD domain separation and the three v3 writers on org data were listed here and are CLOSED by #797 — package C above. `frontend/src/generated/*` was listed as not regenerated and is CLOSED: `quality.yml` runs `npm run codegen && git diff --exit-code` on every PR, so a stale snapshot cannot merge.) `set_actor_llm_tier_ceiling` loosening on a no-2FA credential (product call); webhook POST retry default 3 (pinned contract); Unicode look-alike delimiters.

### The lint checks' regression narratives → [`2026-09-25-lint-check-narratives.md`](docs/engineering-log/2026-09-25-lint-check-narratives.md)

**The class.** A lint check's CLAUDE.md entry carries two things: its
SPECIFICATION (the rule, the scope, the opt-out marker, every measured precision
number, the recorded decisions, the stated limits) and the regression STORY that
motivated it. The specification is why the entry lives in a file read at every
session start; the story is not. Four entries — **74** (report-side swallowed
reads and sub-leg 74b's `Readings` ledger), **88** (static sqlx statements that
must PREPARE), **83** (an explicit `updated_at = NOW()` overriding the trigger's
verdict) and **65** (the dev Prometheus that observed nothing of Talos) — were
compressed to specification only; their stories moved VERBATIM to the archive
file above. Measured: **45,560 → 20,993 bytes, a 54% cut, 24.6 KB off every
session start.**

**DECIDED: the block is now CLOSED for compression, and the estimate that opened
it is REFUTED.** Package EP recorded that the top twelve entries were 51% of the
186 KB block while a check's actual specification is "600–900 B", implying ~85 KB
recoverable. That estimate was taken from the MEDIAN entry (659 B) and does not
survive contact with the large ones, where the scope is a twenty-term name glob,
the precision is four separately-measured populations and the limits are five
enumerated evasions. Measured per entry, worst-first: 74 **56%**, 83 **62%**,
88 **51%**, 65 **45%** — then **79 at 27%** and **85 at 12%**, both of which are
two legs plus a sub-leg with their own numbers and limits, i.e. almost pure
specification. **79 and 85 are therefore deliberately NOT compressed**, and the
remaining 91 entries have a median of 659 B and are already at the specification
floor. So DN's original reason for leaving this block alone ("its inline
documentation IS each check's specification") holds better than EP's
re-measurement suggested; what EP got right is that the four largest carried
story worth moving, and that is now done.

**Stated limit.** What stayed is a judgement about which clause is specification
and which is story, made by the author of the compression. The archive keeps
every original byte-for-byte, so a clause cut in error is recoverable rather than
lost, and structural check 96 proves that mechanically against a pinned base.

## Sub-workflow dispatch (engine)

Every parent node that runs a sub-workflow (judge, ensemble, reflective-retry, llm-dispatch, sub_workflow) uses the shared dispatcher pattern in `controller/src/engine/parallel.rs`:

1. **`execute_subworkflow_graph(wf_id, trigger_input, nats, shared_key)`** — the canonical invocation path. Loads the graph, builds an engine, registers a synthetic `__trigger__` node, runs `run_with_seed`, collapses the output. Returns `Result<JsonValue, SubflowError>`.
2. **`collapse_subworkflow_output(ctx_results, sub_engine)`** — flattens per-node results. Single terminal → its unwrapped output (what parent nodes expect). Multiple terminals → label-keyed map (diamond fallback).
3. **`JudgeVerdict::from_collapsed(&collapsed)`** — types the `{score, passed, reasoning, feedback, not_applicable}` parse; `malformed_field_count` > 0 surfaces bad judge workflows loudly. **Judge verdicts are three-valued.** The optional `not_applicable: bool` (absent → false; present-but-not-bool → false + malformed++) marks a run with NOTHING TO JUDGE — a quiet inbox, an empty batch. Such a verdict is recorded into `judge_scores` as an ABSTENTION ROW (`not_applicable = true`) and excluded from every aggregate structurally (`FILTER (WHERE NOT not_applicable)`), with the count surfaced as `na_runs` beside the scored `runs` — scoring an empty run 1.0 inflates the average and saturates the signal, scoring it 0.2 drags the trend, and simply DROPPING the row destroys the abstention rate ("ran 17×, abstained 12×" became indistinguishable from "ran 5×"). The flag affects RECORDING only, NEVER routing: `passed` drives the gate exactly as authored on all three `on_failure` branches, so an abstaining judge must also set `passed` (usually `true`). Any new reader of `judge_scores` must carry the FILTER, and any renderer of `runs`/`avg_score`/`pass_rate`/`worst_score` must state that its population is scored-only. **Every judge-owned `__judge_*` key is written UNCONDITIONALLY by the single `build_judge_envelope`** (insert-or-REMOVE, never insert-or-inherit): the pass/passthrough branches build on top of the PARENT node's output map, so a conditionally-inserted key is inherited from caller data — an upstream module (or an earlier judge in a chain) emitting `__judge_not_applicable__: true` would otherwise erase the downstream judge's real datapoint from the trend, and `__judge_rejected__: true` would misroute a passing verdict. Same rule as the reserved-key strip on inbound trigger payloads: engine-authored keys are never caller-authorable.
4. **`dispatch_judge / dispatch_subworkflow / dispatch_ensemble / dispatch_reflective_retry / dispatch_llm_dispatch`** — one `&self async fn` per system-node kind, called from BOTH the main `run()` loop and `run_with_seed()`. Never re-inline the dispatch logic — extend the dispatcher.

**A sub-workflow bound to its OWN actor runs AS that actor** (2026-07). `AdapterSet` copies the parent's `actor_id` + ceilings verbatim into a freshly-built sub-engine; `bind_subengine_actor_and_ceilings` then (a) adopts the sub-workflow's own bound actor identity so direct `agent_memory::get/set` RPCs resolve against the sub-workflow's actor — matching the `__actor_context__` injection path, which already used it — and (b) narrows each ceiling to `most_restrictive(parent, sub-actor)`. Identity is adopted verbatim (not a lattice); ceilings only ever tighten. A sub-workflow with NO bound actor keeps the parent identity (reusable judge/classifier utilities run in the caller's context). Before this fix, a child bound to actor B invoked from a parent bound to actor A silently read A's memory (empty for the child's own keys) — the cross-actor recall bridge looked wired but returned nothing. Resolved atomically via `get_workflow_actor_binding` (identity + ceilings in one owner-validated JOIN) → `SubworkflowBinding` → `apply_subworkflow_binding`. Lint check 57 enforces the chokepoint. To bridge cross-actor memory, bind the reading workflow to the target actor (do NOT rely on a sub_workflow node re-scoping for you — it now does the RIGHT thing, but the whole-workflow bind is the simplest correct shape).

Use `test_subworkflow_contract` MCP tool while authoring a judge/reflection/classifier sub-workflow — it runs the same `execute_subworkflow_graph` + `collapse_subworkflow_output` path the parent will use, plus per-contract interpretation (judge verdict parse, class extraction). Catches shape bugs before wiring up.

## LLM key resolution (vault-first everywhere)

Canonical LLM provider vault paths live in **one place**: `job_protocol::LLM_PROVIDER_VAULT_PATHS` + `is_llm_provider_vault_path(path)`. Three consumers import from there:
- `controller/src/engine/parallel.rs::prefetch_llm_vault_keys` — injects keys into every worker job's secrets map so `llm::*` host functions can resolve them.
- `controller/src/secrets/mod.rs::get_llm_vault_keys` — per-user 60s-TTL cache; eagerly invalidated by `create_secret`/`update_secret`/`delete_secret` when path matches. Background sweep task in `main.rs` evicts expired entries every 300s (`LLM_KEYS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SECS`).
- `worker/src/host_impl.rs::check_secret_allowlist` — DENIES these paths from WASM guest code even when `allowed_secrets: ["*"]`. Reserved for host-internal `llm::*` consumption.

Controller-side `LlmClient` uses `with_vault(SecretsManager, env_fallback)` — per-request resolution hits the same cache, so `rotate_secret anthropic/api_key` propagates to controller scaffolding AND worker sandbox LLM calls within one TTL window. Use `LlmClient::new(env_only)` only in tests.

**Adding a new LLM provider** = one change to `job_protocol::LLM_PROVIDER_VAULT_PATHS`. Everything else picks it up automatically. Also update `worker::host_impl::llm_key_lookup_paths` for the `vault_path → env_name` fallback mapping. **And** add the provider's API hostname to `job_protocol::EXTERNAL_LLM_HOSTS` — without this, a tier-1 actor's HTTP-host gate won't deny the new provider's endpoint.

## Per-actor LLM tier ceiling (data-egress privacy gate)

Actors carry a `max_llm_tier` ceiling — `tier1` = local Ollama only (data
must not leave host), `tier2` = external providers allowed (default).
Schema: `actors.max_llm_tier text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'tier2'` (migration
`20260424100000_actors_max_llm_tier.sql`). Operator tool:
`set_actor_llm_tier_ceiling(actor_id, tier)` (writes to `admin_event_log`
on every change).

The tier travels with the job — HMAC-bound in BOTH `JobRequest` AND
`PipelineJobRequest` signing payloads (appended at end per the
wire-format stability rule). An on-wire attacker can't downgrade a
tier-1 ceiling to tier-2 without invalidating the signature.

**`egress_scope` — a SEPARATE axis from `max_llm_tier`** (2026-07). `max_llm_tier`
historically drove BOTH "no external LLM" AND a blanket "no public network egress
at all" (the worker SSRF `local_egress_only`), so a tier-1 actor couldn't reach a
legitimate public API like Gmail even though its LLM was already ollama-pinned
(this broke the inbox organizers when PA went tier-1). `actors.egress_scope`
(`local`|`public`, NULLABLE) decouples them: it overrides ONLY the blanket
public-egress SSRF gate (`worker::context` `resolve_local_egress_only`), leaving
the LLM-provider deny + raw-`wasi:sockets` grant + public-IP-literal deny all
keyed to `max_llm_tier`. `NULL` = tier-derived default (`tier1`→local,
`tier2`→public — byte-identical to pre-split). `Some(Public)` on a `tier1` actor
= reaches declared `allowed_hosts` (Gmail) while STILL refusing external LLM. The
override travels HMAC-bound alongside `max_llm_tier` (conditional-append, so
default-`None` is byte-identical; a `public`↔`local` flip or strip fails
verification), stamped by `apply_actor_to_engine` (fail-closed to `Local`) +
every module-bound dispatch path, and narrowed across sub-workflows via
`EgressScope::narrow` (explicit `Local` on either side wins). Operator tool:
`set_actor_egress_scope(actor_id, scope)` (`admin_event_log`; scope null clears
the override). Core type `talos_workflow_engine_core::EgressScope` (fail-closed
`from_db_str`→`Local`). **The house pattern for a Gmail-reading privacy actor is
`max_llm_tier=tier1` + `egress_scope=public`**, NOT tier-2.

**Five enforcement surfaces in the worker** (all guarded by `self.max_llm_tier == Tier1`):
1. `host_impl::get_llm_api_key` / `get_llm_api_key_by_name` — refuse to resolve external-provider vault keys (the `decide_llm_tier_access` helper centralises this — see `llm_tier_decision_tests`).
2. `host_impl::resolve_vault_header` — refuse `vault://anthropic|openai|gemini/*` substitution into HTTP headers.
3. `wit_http::fetch` + `wit_http::fetch_all` — refuse hosts in `job_protocol::EXTERNAL_LLM_HOSTS` regardless of `allowed_hosts`.
4. `wit_graphql::execute` — same host deny-list.
5. `wit_webhook::send` + `wit_http_stream` — same host deny-list.

**Defense in depth on the controller side:** `build_encrypted_secrets_for` takes `max_llm_tier` and SKIPS the `resolve_llm_keys` prefetch entirely when `Tier1`. Tier-1 jobs never have an Anthropic/OpenAI/Gemini key on the wire (encrypted or otherwise) — bounds blast radius if a future bypass slips.

**Stamping the tier on a workflow execution:** ALWAYS use `talos_engine::actor_binding::apply_actor_to_engine(&actor_repo, &mut engine, actor_id)` (moved from `ActorRepository` in 2026-07 — lint check 51 forbids the repo→engine dep edge) — it sets `actor_id` AND `max_llm_tier` together and fail-closes to Tier-1 on DB error. Never call bare `engine.set_actor_id(aid)` — lint check 29 catches it (the setter is confined to `talos-workflow-engine/` and `talos-engine/src/actor_binding.rs`).

**Module-bound dispatch** (Gmail/GCal/webhook push notifications) is intentionally Tier-2 default — those paths fire individual modules without an owning actor. Operators who need tier-1 enforcement for inbound-event processing wrap the module in a workflow with an actor that has `max_llm_tier=tier1`.

## Secret Handling Rules (CRITICAL — security invariant)

**Plaintext secret values MUST NEVER leave the controller host** except through two audited paths:
1. **Outbound HTTP headers** — `vault://` resolution places the secret into a header for an external API call; the `Zeroizing<String>` is cleared after use.
2. **Tier-2 `expose_secret`** — explicit opt-in per module (`allow_tier2_exposure: true`), rate-limited (10/execution, 100/user/day), audit-logged at WARN level. Currently hardcoded to `false` across all engine dispatch paths.

**Every engine dispatch path MUST call `build_encrypted_secrets()`** (or the equivalent inline block) to populate the job's `encrypted_secrets` field. Sending `Default::default()` means the module silently loses access to all secrets — vault:// headers fail with `Notfound`, LLM calls fail with missing keys. This was a real bug in loop-node dispatches fixed 2026-04-16. When adding a new dispatch path (new system-node kind, new parallel executor, etc.), grep for `encrypted_secrets:` in `parallel.rs` and verify the new site matches the existing pattern.

**Secret flow through the system:**
- Controller: `SecretsManager::get_module_secrets(node_id)` + `get_secrets_by_paths(vault_paths)` + `prefetch_llm_vault_keys(user_id)` → plaintext `HashMap<String, String>` → `EncryptedSecrets::encrypt(map, key)` → AES-256-GCM ciphertext in `JobRequest.encrypted_secrets` → NATS publish.
- Worker: `EncryptedSecrets::decrypt(key)` → plaintext `HashMap` loaded into `SecretProvider` DashMap → WASM guest receives opaque `u64` handle only (Tier-1), never the string.
- No MCP handler, GraphQL query, or REST endpoint returns plaintext secret values. `get_secret` is internal-only. MCP is **read-only for secrets** (MCP-1201): `set_secret` / `delete_secret` / `set_secret_namespace` / `set_secret_expiry` / `rotate_secret` were removed because MCP API keys are long-lived bearer tokens with no 2FA equivalent — secret writes would have bypassed the `require_2fa + SecretsWrite` discipline the GraphQL surface enforces. Mutations go through `talos-api/src/schema/secrets/mutations.rs`; MCP retains the read surface (list, namespaces, usage, health, normalize). `refresh_oauth_token` is the lone MCP write that touches vault — provider-side token rotation, no MCP-supplied value crosses the boundary. The GraphQL `Secret` type has no `value` field.
- DLP `redact_json()` is applied to module execution output before DB storage (catches `sk-*`, `ghp_*`, Bearer tokens, etc.).
- Audit logs record `key_hash` (SHA-256 of path), never the value.
- Error messages reference `key_path`/`name` only, never decrypted content.

**Per-context AEAD subkeys + per-ORG root DEKs (formats v3/v4).** Every AES-GCM
path derives a PER-CONTEXT key — `HKDF-SHA256(ikm = a DEK, salt = label, info =
aad_context)` — rather than encrypting many rows under one shared key (keeps the
per-key message count ~1, so the random-96-bit-nonce birthday bound is
unreachable). **There are two DEK scopes** (`encryption_keys.org_id`): one
**global** DEK (`org_id IS NULL`) and one **per-organization** root DEK per org
(`org_id` set; exactly one active per org, lazily provisioned on first use). Both
partial-unique-indexed. So:
- **v3** = per-context subkey from the GLOBAL DEK.
- **v4** = the SAME derivation but from the writer-org's root DEK — so a
  compromised root key is bounded to one tenant, not the whole system.

DB-backed paths (`SecretsManager`: secrets / actor_memory / TOTP / webhook
secrets / exec output / module payloads) write **v4** when an org is resolvable,
falling back to **v3 (global)** for legitimately org-less rows (personal secrets,
standalone module executions). **Decrypt is IDENTICAL for v3 and v4** — the row's
`*_key_id` names the DEK (`get_dek` resolves global-or-org by id) and the subkey
re-derives from the same AAD; `decrypt_versioned` dispatches v0/v1/v2/v3/v4 on the
per-row `*_format` column. Only ENCRYPT differs (which DEK is the IKM).

**Adding a new AEAD writer?** Call `encrypt_value_aad_v4_or_global(value,
target_org, aad)` and resolve `target_org` from context — the workflow's org for
executions, the actor's org for memory, the secret's `org_id`. For
personal/user-keyed tables use `encrypt_value_aad_v4_for_user(value, user_id,
aad)` (resolves the user's personal org). **Bind the RETURNED format version,
never hardcode 3/4** (a v4 row mislabeled v3 fails to decrypt), and widen that
table's `*_format` CHECK to include 4. If a table has its OWN decrypt dispatch
(e.g. `decrypt_secret_record`) add the v4 arm there too, and guard any global
re-encrypt sweep to skip `format = 4` (don't downgrade org rows).
**A row sealed across more than one write** (a `module_executions` row seals input at start and output at completion under one shared key id and format) must seal every later slot under the key and format the row ALREADY names — `SecretsManager::encrypt_value_aad_under_row_key`, via `encrypt_output_for_row` — never a freshly resolved one (package CJ).

**Migrating EXISTING rows to per-org** (the cutover only converts NEW writes):
per-table sweeps `SecretsManager::re_encrypt_*_to_org` /
`ModuleExecutionService::re_encrypt_module_payloads_to_org` /
`talos_memory::re_encrypt_memories_to_org`, exposed as platform-admin mutations
`reEncrypt{Secrets,Memories,Outputs,ModulePayloads}ToOrg`. Poll the
`dekMigrationStatus` query until each `pending` is 0 → the global DEK is no longer
load-bearing for migratable data (org-less rows stay global by design). The
personal tables (totp/webhook/audit) have no sweep — they migrate lazily on next
write.

**Separate, NON-DEK derivations (NOT per-org, do not "fix"):** checkpoints fold
`execution_id` from the `WORKER_SHARED_KEY` (`checkpoint-aead/v2-per-execution`);
the worker secret-envelope folds the per-job AAD from the WSK
(`envelope-aead/v2-per-job`); OTLP headers fold `user_id`. These don't use the DEK
at all, so they're already isolated from a DEK compromise.

**Envelope deploy ordering:** workers must roll first/together with controllers —
a v1-only worker can't open a v2-sealed envelope (the v2→v1 decrypt fallback only
covers the reverse). New AEAD format versions need the format CHECK widened in a
migration (`20260617120000` introduced v3; the `2026062612*`–`2026062624*` set
introduced per-org v4 per table).

**Worker-side secret isolation:**
- `check_secret_allowlist(key_path)` enforces BOTH the per-module `allowed_secrets` grant AND the host-reserved deny-list (`is_reserved_host_secret_path`). The deny-list blocks LLM provider keys even with `allowed_secrets: ["*"]`.
- The allowlist matcher lives in ONE place: `job_protocol::vault_path_permitted`. Both controller (validation) and worker (runtime enforcement) import from there.

## Security Rules (MUST follow)
- NEVER log sensitive values (tokens, cookies, API keys, secrets). Log presence only.
- NEVER return internal error details to API clients. Log full errors server-side, return generic messages.
- NEVER fall back to plaintext credential storage in production. Require SecretsManager.
- NEVER store secrets unencrypted in the database. Use envelope encryption via SecretsManager.
- NEVER send `encrypted_secrets: Default::default()` in a dispatch path that should have secrets — use `build_encrypted_secrets()`.
- NEVER modify already-applied migration files. Create new migrations instead.
- ALWAYS use parameterized queries (sqlx `$1` bind params). Never string-concatenate SQL.
- ALWAYS use constant-time comparison for security-sensitive values (tokens, HMAC, CSRF).
- ALWAYS set HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict on authentication cookies.
- ALWAYS validate and sanitize external input at API boundaries.
- ALWAYS cap resource consumption (timeouts, memory limits, rate limits) for untrusted inputs.

## Performance Rules
- NEVER use N+1 query patterns. Batch with `WHERE id = ANY($1)` when processing collections.
- NEVER use unbounded in-memory collections. Set explicit size limits and eviction policies.
- ALWAYS add database indexes for frequently queried column combinations.
- Use `CREATE INDEX` (not `CONCURRENTLY`) in migration files (sqlx runs in transactions).

## Engine retry & wire-format rules (learned 2026-07-24, PRs #566–#570)

- **`modules.max_retries = 0` means "unset / no opinion", NOT "explicit no-retry".** Nothing writes that column, so its DB default (0) reaches every node. Do NOT stamp it verbatim onto nodes and do NOT "simplify" it to a blanket constant — resolve it through `talos_workflow_engine_core::default_max_retries_for_module(allowed_methods, capability_world)` (via `NodeTemplateRow::effective_max_retries()` at creation and the engine's absent-policy fallback). The default is **method-aware**: read-only / pure-compute worlds (minimal/secrets, or http/agent with a DECLARED GET/HEAD-only `allowed_methods`) get transient retries; governance/messaging/database/unknown worlds and state-changing HTTP fail closed to 0. **An EMPTY `allowed_methods` is UNKNOWN, not read-only** — the worker's three enforcement points (`host/http.rs` fetch + fetch_all, `host/graphql.rs`) read empty as "allow every verb", so an undeclared module can POST and must not earn a blind retry; `.all()` over an empty slice is vacuously true, which is how it used to read. Note the asymmetry: empty `allowed_hosts` and empty `allowed_secrets` both DENY all; `allowed_methods` is the only one of the three where empty means allow. A blanket-0 default made the entire retry machinery dead fleet-wide (the 2026-07-23 outage failed ~125 read-only fetches that each ran exactly once). Explicit per-node `retry_count` (including 0) always wins. Idempotent sends opt back into retries via `__idempotency_key__` → `effective_retries_with_idempotency` (HTTP-egress worlds only).
- **Correction to the line above, 2026-09-24 (package EJ): an empty `allowed_methods` now DENIES every verb.** The base line is left byte-identical for `scripts/check-engineering-log.py`'s losslessness leg, which is why this is a separate line. Two clauses of it are now false: the enforcement points are **FIVE**, not three (`host/http.rs` fetch + fetch_all, `host/graphql.rs`, `host/webhook.rs`, and `host/http_stream.rs`, the last added by EJ because an ungated SSE connect would have left an undeclared module able to egress); and they no longer "read empty as 'allow every verb'" — `talos_workflow_job_protocol::method_permitted` denies on empty, so `allowed_methods` is no longer the odd one out and the stated asymmetry with `allowed_hosts`/`allowed_secrets` is CLOSED. What is UNCHANGED is the retry verdict: `methods_are_read_only` still returns `false` on empty and an undeclared module still earns 0 retries — for the opposite reason, that it makes no HTTP call at all, so there is nothing for a transient retry to re-send.
- **Pipeline steps retry too.** `execute_pipeline`'s per-step loop honors per-step `max_retries` gated by the transient classifier — do not re-hardcode step `max_retries: 0`. The transient classifier must match BOTH `"timeout"` AND `"timed out"` (the worker's own step-timeout message uses the latter).
- **The attempt-window arithmetic has ONE home: `talos_workflow_engine_core::attempt_window` (2026-09-06).** How much of a workflow's wall-clock budget one dispatch attempt may occupy is asked on two surfaces — the DISPATCHER (`talos_workflow_engine_nats::execute_job_with_retry`, where a wrong answer is a real cancellation) and the VALIDATOR (`talos_workflow_validation::retry_envelope_overrun`, where a wrong answer is advice an operator acts on). They had two implementations. The dispatcher's: `min(allowance, remaining − BUDGET_RESERVE_SECS(2))`, where `allowance = timeout_secs + TOKIO_WRAP_GRACE_SECS(5)`. The validator's: `envelope_secs <= budget_secs`. **They disagree by 7 s at the boundary**, so a node configured at 120 s inside a 120 s budget was reported as fitting and clamped to 117 s (`as_secs()` truncation) on attempt 1 of every run — measured live on the dev fleet: **9 such nodes across 3 ACTIVE workflows, 2 326 clamped attempts per 48 h** (`pa-ask-email` 1848, `pa-followup-approval-notifier` 382, `ops-critical-notifier` 96), every one on `attempt=1` of a run that then completed in under a second. `clamp_attempt_timeout`, `AttemptWindow` and the three constants MOVED to core (they are not copies — the dispatcher re-imports, `dispatch_allowance_secs` states the `+ 5` once), and the validator now SIMULATES the configured attempt sequence through the same `attempt_window_for_remaining`. Do not re-derive either half.
  - **Three outcomes, and the middle one was unsayable before.** `AttemptFit::Full` (silent) / `Clamped` (every attempt starts, at least one is cut short — a real finding, lower severity, category `attempt-window-clamped`) / `Truncated` (an attempt is never dispatched — the historical `retry-envelope` category). Fleet effect, measured: the old check reported **7** nodes; the new one reports **16** — 4 truncated (a strict SUBSET of the old 7) and 12 clamped, of which 3 were previously reported as the SEVERE finding (correctly downgraded: one 4 x 120 s node in a 450 s budget gets all four of its attempts, the fourth cut to 38 s) and 9 were reported as nothing at all. `ValidationSeverity` has only `Error`/`Warning`, so "lower severity" is expressed in the category and the wording; adding an `Info` variant would move every counter and response shape that reads a `ValidationResult` and was deliberately NOT done. `max_retries_within_budget` searches the same simulation, so it stays the exact inverse — and it MOVED by one retry on shapes the old formula's slack fitted an extra attempt into (`(120, 500, 240)`: 1 → 0).
  - **The prose in BOTH crates was one release behind the code.** `describe_retry_envelope_overrun` and `talos-mcp-handlers`' `describe_retry_bound` both said *"the retry loop has no view of the workflow deadline … the whole execution is dropped — discarding every sibling node that had already finished"*. False since #686 (2026-08-27, the same day that text was written): a clamped attempt that times out, and an attempt refused for want of budget, are both ORDINARY NODE FAILURES the engine routes (error edges, `continue_on_error`, DLQ) with sibling results kept. A single-string grep finds only ONE of the two — the handler's copy is reworded — so a fix to one crate really is a drift.
  - **The residual, stated rather than dropped.** The budget is still an OUTER `tokio::time::timeout` that drops the reactor future, and `BUDGET_RESERVE_SECS` makes the failure RECORDING likely, not certain: `handle_node_failure` awaits a `node_failed` INSERT, the DLQ write and a sibling reap, and a slower failure path still loses the race. The clamp covers module dispatch ONLY — `sub_workflow`/judge/ensemble nodes awaited inline are unclamped (the validator skips `system:*` nodes, so it claims nothing about them), and **`engine_dispatch_pipeline.rs` passes `deadline: None`**, so the chain path is unclamped too — dormant by config (`ChainDispatch::Disabled` at every production entry point) and RECORDED, not fixed.
